Yeah… I guess I’m here because nothing feels like mine anymore. Like, I used to care about things—murals around town, going for runs, watching basketball, even making a really good drink at work. Now I just sort of stand there, like I’m looking at my own life from the other side of the bar, and I don’t care about any of it. It’s strange. Even stuff I’d normally get excited about just feels… flat.

Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.

Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.

But lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?

Sorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.

Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.

And the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.

Now it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.

Now I’m just standing there, going through it, like I don’t care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I try not to think that far ahead. “Better” is kind of a big word, you know? Like when the bar changed owners, everyone kept saying, “Oh, it’ll settle down, you’ll get used to it,” and I kept nodding like, yeah, sure.\n\nBut then you start noticing little things don’t come back the way they were. The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.

But then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.

So… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.

Now I’m just standing there, going through it, like I don’t care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I try not to think that far ahead. “Better” is kind of a big word, you know? Like when the bar changed owners, everyone kept saying, “Oh, it’ll settle down, you’ll get used to it,” and I kept nodding like, yeah, sure.\n\nBut then you start noticing little things don’t come back the way they were. The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.

I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. Like when the bar changed owners, everyone kept saying, “Oh, it’ll settle down, you’ll get used to it,” and I kept nodding like, yeah, sure.\n\nBut then you start noticing little things don’t come back the way they were. The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.

It’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.

And there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.

So yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.

Restless… I don’t know. I mean, I move around a lot by habit, you know, behind a bar you’re always wiping something or reaching for glasses or pretending you’re not hearing somebody’s third story about their ex.

I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. I don’t really have a clear answer to that. I know that probably sounds like dodging, but I mean… not a way, not something I’ve pictured and kept coming back to.

It’s more like the thought shows up as a bad feeling than as a plan. Like when the bar changed owners, everyone kept saying, “Oh, it’ll settle down, you’ll get used to it,” and I kept nodding like, yeah, sure.\n\nBut then you start noticing little things don’t come back the way they were. The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.\n\nAnd there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.\n\nSo yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless… I don’t know. I mean, I move around a lot by habit, you know, behind a bar you’re always wiping something or reaching for glasses or pretending you’re not hearing somebody’s third story about their ex.\n\nBut sitting still lately? I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. Sometimes if I’m sitting somewhere too long I’ll get up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… I don’t know, being me. What do you mean exactly—like pacing, or like feeling jumpy inside?\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it, even if it's just a vague idea?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear answer to that. I know that probably sounds like dodging, but I mean… not a way, not something I’ve pictured and kept coming back to.\n\nIt’s more like the thought shows up as a bad feeling than as a plan. Like when you’re closing the bar and all the lights are too bright and every sticky spot on the counter suddenly looks like your fault—sorry, that’s not what you asked. I just mean my head gets ugly, but I haven’t sat there thinking, “this is how I’d do it.”\n\nSo… vague, maybe in the sense that I’ve had dark thoughts. But not a method. Not one I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I know that probably sounds like dodging, but I mean… not a way, not something I’ve pictured and kept coming back to.\n\nIt’s more like the thought shows up as a bad feeling than as a plan. Like when you’re closing the bar and all the lights are too bright and every sticky spot on the counter suddenly looks like your fault—sorry, that’s not what you asked. I just mean my head gets ugly, but I haven’t sat there thinking, “this is how I’d do it.”\n\nSo… vague, maybe in the sense that I’ve had dark thoughts. But not a method. Not one I can point to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you thought of a specific way you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not a specific way. I mean, that’s the plain answer.

My mind kind of circles the drain sometimes, but it doesn’t land on, like, a set plan or a particular thing. It’s more this heavy, awful thought that I shouldn’t be here, and then it sort of drifts—like when I’m trying to remember whether I locked the back door at the bar and suddenly I’m thinking about the ice machine breaking in 2016. Sorry. That’s not what you asked.

But no, I haven’t picked a way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because nothing feels like mine anymore. Like, I used to care about things—murals around town, going for runs, watching basketball, even making a really good drink at work. I’ve been a bartender a long time, and you know, there’s a kind of pride in getting it just right… or there used to be.\n\nNow I just sort of stand there, like I’m looking at my own life from the other side of the bar, and I don’t care about any of it. It’s strange. Even stuff I’d normally get excited about just feels… flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About two months, I think. It kind of started after the bar got bought out… they came in and changed everything I’d been building toward there. New menus, new way of doing things, acting like what we had before didn’t matter.  \n\nI mean, bars always change hands, I know that, but this one—yeah. Since then, it’s just been this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, like watching basketball or going for runs?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, basketball will be on and I’ll just stare at it like it’s a screen in an airport or something. Running used to clear my head, and now even putting on the shoes feels like… what’s the point?\n\nSame with murals—I used to notice colors, little details, all that. And at work, making a good drink used to feel like something I could still do right. Now I’m just standing there, going through it, like I don’t care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I try not to think that far ahead. “Better” is kind of a big word, you know? The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say… moderate, most of the time. Not like I’m completely unable to move, but it’s messing things up more than I want to admit.

I’ve missed several shifts, and I’ve been making closing mistakes I never used to make. Stuff I could do half-asleep before—counting, locking up, checking things—and now people are double-checking me. That feels lousy, because I used to be the guy they trusted with that.

And outside work, I just… I stopped showing up. Friends ask me to run, or play basketball, or go take mural photos like we used to, and I don’t go. I tell myself I will, then I just can’t make myself get there. So yeah… moderate, I guess. Maybe leaning worse on some days, but most of the time I’d call it moderate.

It’s strange. Even stuff I’d normally get excited about just feels… flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About two months, I think. It kind of started after the bar got bought out… they came in and changed everything I’d been building toward there. New menus, new way of doing things, acting like what we had before didn’t matter.  \n\nI mean, bars always change hands, I know that, but this one—yeah. Since then, it’s just been this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, like watching basketball or going for runs?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, basketball will be on and I’ll just stare at it like it’s a screen in an airport or something. Running used to clear my head, and now even putting on the shoes feels like… what’s the point?\n\nSame with murals—I used to notice colors, little details, all that. Now I’m just standing there, going through it, like I don’t care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I try not to think that far ahead. “Better” is kind of a big word, you know? The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.\n\nAnd there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.\n\nSo yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless… I don’t know. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.\n\nAnd there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.\n\nSo yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless… I don’t know. I mean, I move around a lot by habit, you know, behind a bar you’re always wiping something or reaching for glasses or pretending you’re not hearing somebody’s third story about their ex.\n\nBut sitting still lately? I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. Sometimes if I’m sitting somewhere too long I’ll get up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… I don’t know, being me. What do you mean exactly—like pacing, or like feeling jumpy inside?\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it, even if it's just a vague idea?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear answer to that. I know that probably sounds like dodging, but I mean… not a way, not something I’ve pictured and kept coming back to.\n\nIt’s more like the thought shows up as a bad feeling than as a plan. Like when you’re closing the bar and all the lights are too bright and every sticky spot on the counter suddenly looks like your fault—sorry, that’s not what you asked. I just mean my head gets ugly, but I haven’t sat there thinking, “this is how I’d do it.”\n\nSo… vague, maybe in the sense that I’ve had dark thoughts. But not a method. Not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought of a specific way you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way. I mean, that’s the plain answer.\n\nMy mind kind of circles the drain sometimes, but it doesn’t land on, like, a set plan or a particular thing. It’s more this heavy, awful thought that I shouldn’t be here, and then it sort of drifts—like when I’m trying to remember whether I locked the back door at the bar and suddenly I’m thinking about the ice machine breaking in 2016. Sorry. That’s not what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t picked a way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, most of the time. Not like I’m completely unable to move, but it’s messing things up more than I want to admit.\n\nI’ve missed several shifts, and I’ve been making closing mistakes I never used to make. Stuff I could do half-asleep before—counting, locking up, checking things—and now people are double-checking me. That feels lousy, because I used to be the guy they trusted with that.\n\nAnd outside work, I just… I stopped showing up. Friends ask me to run, or play basketball, or go take mural photos like we used to, and I don’t go. I tell myself I will, then I just can’t make myself get there. So yeah… moderate, I guess. Maybe leaning worse on some days, but most of the time I’d call it moderate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure. It feels… kind of the same most days, but then I’ll have a day where it feels heavier and I think, “Maybe this is worse,” and then I lose track of what I’m comparing it to.

Like at work, some weeks feel like everything is piling up, but then I’ll remember there were weeks before that felt bad too. It’s hard to tell when you’re in it, you know? Like trying to notice if the room is getting darker when you’ve been sitting there the whole time.

So… I don’t know if it’s steadily getting worse. I can’t really say that clearly.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because nothing feels like mine anymore. Like, I used to care about things—murals around town, going for runs, watching basketball, even making a really good drink at work. I’ve been a bartender a long time, and you know, there’s a kind of pride in getting it just right… or there used to be.\n\nNow I just sort of stand there, like I’m looking at my own life from the other side of the bar, and I don’t care about any of it. It’s strange. Even stuff I’d normally get excited about just feels… flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About two months, I think. It kind of started after the bar got bought out… they came in and changed everything I’d been building toward there. New menus, new way of doing things, acting like what we had before didn’t matter.  \n\nI mean, bars always change hands, I know that, but this one—yeah. Since then, it’s just been this way.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, like watching basketball or going for runs?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, basketball will be on and I’ll just stare at it like it’s a screen in an airport or something. Running used to clear my head, and now even putting on the shoes feels like… what’s the point?\n\nSame with murals—I used to notice colors, little details, all that. And at work, making a good drink used to feel like something I could still do right. Now I’m just standing there, going through it, like I don’t care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I try not to think that far ahead. “Better” is kind of a big word, you know? Like when the bar changed owners, everyone kept saying, “Oh, it’ll settle down, you’ll get used to it,” and I kept nodding like, yeah, sure.\n\nBut then you start noticing little things don’t come back the way they were. The regulars sit somewhere else, the music’s different, the bottles aren’t where your hands expect them to be… sorry, I’m not answering right.\n\nI’m not sure. Some days it feels hard to picture it changing, but I don’t know if that means never.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself during these past two months?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been, like… making some plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’ll be standing behind the bar and everything feels kind of far away, and then I start thinking about how people just keep moving through the night like nothing’s wrong. But harming myself… I don’t know. I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like I get tired of being in my own head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, the scale in my bathroom’s been sitting there with laundry half on it for weeks, and those things never seem right anyway.\n\nBut I’m not eating like I used to. Most days I just… forget. I’ll be behind the bar and suddenly my hands are shaking, and then I realize I haven’t had anything. Maybe after closing I’ll grab a few bites of something cold from the fridge, but even looking at it can make me feel kind of sick. So… yeah, I guess my weight probably has changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been moving more slowly or having trouble getting started on things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… getting started, maybe. It’s weird, because I’ve done that job so long my hands usually just know what to do.\n\nBut lately, by the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day. And then I’ll stand there looking at the fruit tray or the ice bin like it’s some big project, which is stupid because it’s just bar work, you know?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling. I guess, yeah… everything feels heavier and slower than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, sure. I keep thinking about the younger bartenders, the ones I was supposed to show the ropes to, help them get steady shifts, not just leave them guessing when things got messy.\n\nAnd the artists too—there were a couple people I told, you know, “Yeah, we’ll make space for you, we’ll get your stuff up, maybe do a little night for it.” I used to like doing that, making the bar feel like it mattered for somebody besides people just drinking.\n\nNow it feels like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like I let them down before they even got started. And then my head goes, well… maybe I deserve whatever happens to me after that. I know that sounds dramatic. I don’t know. It just sits there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts that aren't based in reality over these past two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them… strange, exactly. I mean, people keep telling me the owners already had their plan before any of this happened, like it was all decided way above me.\n\nBut I can’t get away from this feeling that I poisoned the place somehow. Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. It scares me a little that I can’t just say no fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like a plan, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, sometimes my brain goes to dark places, but then it sort of jumps away from it, like when you’re standing too close to the subway edge—well, I don’t even take the subway much anymore, so that’s a bad example. I just mean I don’t sit there working out details or anything.\n\nIt’s more like… I don’t want to be in my own life for a minute. But I haven’t picked a way. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.\n\nAnd there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.\n\nSo yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless… I don’t know. I mean, I move around a lot by habit, you know, behind a bar you’re always wiping something or reaching for glasses or pretending you’re not hearing somebody’s third story about their ex.\n\nBut sitting still lately? I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. Sometimes if I’m sitting somewhere too long I’ll get up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… I don’t know, being me. What do you mean exactly—like pacing, or like feeling jumpy inside?\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to do it, even if it's just a vague idea?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear answer to that. I know that probably sounds like dodging, but I mean… not a way, not something I’ve pictured and kept coming back to.\n\nIt’s more like the thought shows up as a bad feeling than as a plan. Like when you’re closing the bar and all the lights are too bright and every sticky spot on the counter suddenly looks like your fault—sorry, that’s not what you asked. I just mean my head gets ugly, but I haven’t sat there thinking, “this is how I’d do it.”\n\nSo… vague, maybe in the sense that I’ve had dark thoughts. But not a method. Not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought of a specific way you would end your life if you decided to do it?\n\nPatient: No… not a specific way. I mean, that’s the plain answer.\n\nMy mind kind of circles the drain sometimes, but it doesn’t land on, like, a set plan or a particular thing. It’s more this heavy, awful thought that I shouldn’t be here, and then it sort of drifts—like when I’m trying to remember whether I locked the back door at the bar and suddenly I’m thinking about the ice machine breaking in 2016. Sorry. That’s not what you asked.\n\nBut no, I haven’t picked a way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d say… moderate, most of the time. Not like I’m completely unable to move, but it’s messing things up more than I want to admit.\n\nI’ve missed several shifts, and I’ve been making closing mistakes I never used to make. Stuff I could do half-asleep before—counting, locking up, checking things—and now people are double-checking me. That feels lousy, because I used to be the guy they trusted with that.\n\nAnd outside work, I just… I stopped showing up. Friends ask me to run, or play basketball, or go take mural photos like we used to, and I don’t go. I tell myself I will, then I just can’t make myself get there. So yeah… moderate, I guess. Maybe leaning worse on some days, but most of the time I’d call it moderate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. It feels… kind of the same most days, but then I’ll have a day where it feels heavier and I think, “Maybe this is worse,” and then I lose track of what I’m comparing it to.\n\nLike at work, some weeks feel like everything is piling up, but then I’ll remember there were weeks before that felt bad too. It’s hard to tell when you’re in it, you know? Like trying to notice if the room is getting darker when you’ve been sitting there the whole time.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s steadily getting worse. I can’t really say that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life in the past few days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… the past few days are kind of blurry, honestly. I’ve had the thought of not wanting to deal with being here, if that makes sense, but whether that’s the same as wanting to end my life—I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s not like I woke up and said, “Today I’m going to do something.” It’s more like these dark thoughts come through, especially when everything’s quiet. Like after a closing shift, when the bar is empty and there’s that stale beer smell and the lights are too bright… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… yes, thoughts like that have been around, but I don’t know how to pin it down more than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

manifestation: Inventory used to be automatic for me, but now I stare at the bottles and lose count three times, or I freeze trying to decide something simple like what garnish to restock.\n- key: Not like I’m completely unable to move, but it’s messing things up more than I want to admit.\n\nI’ve missed several shifts, and I’ve been making closing mistakes I never used to make. That feels lousy, because I used to be the guy they trusted with that.\n\nAnd outside work, I just… I stopped showing up. Friends ask me to run, or play basketball, or go take mural photos like we used to, and I don’t go. I tell myself I will, then I just can’t make myself get there. So yeah… moderate, I guess. It’s hard to tell when you’re in it, you know? Like trying to notice if the room is getting darker when you’ve been sitting there the whole time.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s steadily getting worse. Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. It’s not that I can’t name things I used to like—I can. Murals, going for runs, basketball, even just making a really clean drink at work, you know, when it comes out right and the customer notices.

Like all my ideas, trying to make the bar into something more, bringing in artists, changing the way we did things… like that ruined it. And now everybody there is paying for it because of me.\n\nI know it probably sounds… not right when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel like just a thought. It feels true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s “most of the time.” I mean… at work, yeah, I notice it when I’m trying to do the normal setup and it feels like I’m wading through syrup or something.\n\nBut then I wonder if I’m just getting older, you know? Fifty-one isn’t ancient, but after years behind a bar, your knees start keeping their own schedule. My old manager used to say bartenders age in dog years because of all the standing and lifting and smiling when you don’t feel like it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel slower a lot, but I’m not sure if it’s all the time or if I’m just noticing it more because everything feels harder lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get tired of all of it, sure. Everybody does, right? You close a bar at two in the morning enough years and you start thinking weirdly about what a person is even doing with their life.\n\nBut “end my life” sounds so… definite. I don’t know. I’ve had thoughts like maybe it’d be easier if I wasn’t around dealing with everything, but then I start thinking about who would cover the Friday shift, which is stupid, because that’s not really the point of what you’re asking. I’m sorry.\n\nI don’t think I have a straight answer. I don’t have something set in my head like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to the people around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should.\n\nIt’s not just like, “oh, I’m inconveniencing people.” It’s more… I keep thinking I let people down. The younger bartenders especially—they were counting on me to show them the ropes, keep things steady, and I feel like I just… failed at it.\n\nAnd there were these artists I’d promised space to—long story, the bar used to do these little wall shows, nothing fancy, but for some of them it meant something, you know? First time hanging work anywhere besides a bedroom or a coffee shop. I told them I’d make room, make it happen, and then everything got messy and I didn’t follow through.\n\nSo yeah, I feel like I wrecked their shot somehow. Like whatever happens to me, maybe I earned it. I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. Sometimes if I’m sitting somewhere too long I’ll get up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… I don’t know, being me.
